The $-100$ death penalty amplifies DAF; environments with softer penalties would exhibit lower DAF values (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). The 19 excluded task types (63\\%) lack routing signal because both strategies typically time out within 30 steps; increasing the step budget could surface additional signal. Our internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}; more sophisticated strategies (e.g., Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\\citep{tot-2023}) may reduce internal death rates and lower DAF. Episode-level labels may assign suboptimal routing to individual steps, particularly those preceding death events, and training on pure-strategy trajectories introduces distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}. Cross-environment analyses on MATH and ALFWorld (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}, Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-env}) provide supporting evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, $N{=}70$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}) confirms direction-consistent death patterns for all tasks, though both models are in the 7--8B parameter range; validation at larger scales remains future work.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a publicly available text-based simulation environment, and does not involve human subjects or private data. All language models are accessed via standard APIs or publicly released weights. We report computational costs: approximately 40 GPU-hours on two consumer GPUs for data collection, router training, and evaluation. Code and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\n\\section{Labeling Quality Audit}\n\\label{app:labeling}\n\nA potential concern is oracle circularity: whether episode-level labels reflect tie-breaking artifacts rather than genuine modality differences. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \\texttt{no\\_preference} labels (61.2\\%), 115 (46.9\\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\\%) are both-dead---all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. Retraining the router using only death-asymmetry labels yields step-level balanced accuracy of 57.3\\%, within 1$\\sigma$ of the full-label retrain (57.6 $\\pm$ 0.5\\%, step-level Trainer evaluation), confirming that score-comparison labels contribute negligible additional signal. (Step-level metrics are used here for the relative comparison; as shown in \\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}, absolute generalization to new episodes is near chance.) The 97.4\\% step-level tie rate (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}) further motivates episode-level over step-level labeling.\n\n\\section{Per-Task Decomposition}\n\\label{app:per-task}\nWe select 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types via prescreening: task types where both strategies complete at least one episode within the 30-step budget are retained; types where both strategies time out on all seed variations are excluded.\\footnote{Prescreening ensures that the counterfactual comparison is meaningful---task types where neither strategy makes progress provide no routing signal.}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\scriptsize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{2.5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nTask & $n$ & $\\Delta_\\text{full}$ & D\\%\\textsubscript{int} & D\\%\\textsubscript{det} & D / I / N \\\\\n\\midrule\nid-life-stages-1       & 20  & $+47.50$ & 55   & 10   & 9 / 0 / 11  \\\\\nlifespan-long          & 25  & $+44.00$ & 56   & 16   & 11 / 1 / 13 \\\\\nlifespan-l-then-s      & 20  & $+40.80$ & 45   & 10   & 8 / 1 / 11  \\\\\ninclined-plane         & 20  & $+36.00$ & 55   & 20   & 7 / 0 / 13  \\\\\nfreeze                 & 20  & $+30.50$ & 55   & 25   & 6 / 0 / 14  \\\\\nmeas-melt-point        & 20  & $+30.05$ & 55   & 25   & 6 / 0 / 14  \\\\\nid-life-stages-2       & 20  & $+30.00$ & 50   & 20   & 6 / 0 / 14  \\\\\nfind-animal            & 20  & $+26.65$ & 50   & 25   & 5 / 0 / 15  \\\\\ngrow-fruit             & 20  & $+25.10$ & 45   & 20   & 6 / 1 / 13  \\\\\nchem-mix               & 20  & $+21.80$ & 45   & 25   & 6 / 2 / 12  \\\\\n\\midrule\nfind-non-living$^\\dagger$ & 40 & $+9.58$ & 0  & 0    & 16 / 4 / 20 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{All (11 tasks)} & \\textbf{245} & $\\mathbf{+29.60}$ & \\textbf{42.9} & \\textbf{16.3} & \\textbf{86 / 9 / 150} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Per-task decomposition of the death avoidance effect. D\\%: death rate for internal (int) and deterministic (det) strategies. D/I/N: episode labels (det-preferred / internal-preferred / no-preference). Sorted by $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ descending. $\\dagger$\\,Only task with zero deaths under both strategies; $n{=}40$ because data was collected in two batches, with the second batch adding 20 episodes to increase statistical power for this zero-death outlier.}\n\\label{tab:per-task}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Task selection and anti-cherry-picking.}\nThe 11 included task types are those exhibiting meaningful routing signal in prescreening, not those with the highest death rates. The deterministic-strategy death rate is comparable between included (17.8\\%) and excluded (14.9\\%) task types, confirming that prescreening does not select for death-prone tasks. Rather, the key distinction is the \\emph{presence} of routing signal: all 11 included tasks have $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} \\neq 0$ (non-zero performance gap on surviving episodes), while 53\\% of excluded tasks show $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 0$---neither strategy outperforms the other even when both survive, typically because both time out before making progress. \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} serves as an internal counterexample: it has zero deaths under both strategies yet is included because it exhibits non-death routing signal ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +9.58$ entirely from score differences), and deterministic is not always preferred (4 of 20 non-tie labels favor internal).\n\n\\section{Correlation Robustness}\n\\label{app:correlation}\n\nThe Pearson $r = 0.9698$ reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp} is robust across multiple diagnostics. Spearman rank correlation is $\\rho = 0.986$ ($p = 2.4 \\times 10^{-8}$), confirming that the relationship is not driven by outliers or non-linearity. A nonparametric bootstrap (10{,}000 resamples) yields 95\\% CI $[0.952, 0.995]$.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lr@{}}\n\\toprule\nTask removed & $r_{\\text{LOO}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nid-life-stages-1       & 0.963 \\\\\nlifespan-long          & 0.969 \\\\\nlifespan-l-then-s      & 0.976 \\\\\ninclined-plane         & 0.969 \\\\\nfreeze                 & 0.971 \\\\\nmeas-melt-point        & 0.972 \\\\\nid-life-stages-2       & 0.972 \\\\\nfind-animal            & 0.970 \\\\\ngrow-fruit             & 0.973 \\\\\nchem-mix               & 0.969 \\\\\nfind-non-living        & 0.980 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Leave-one-out Pearson $r$ for the death rate differential vs.\\ $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ correlation. Full $r = 0.970$; LOO range $[0.963, 0.980]$. No single task type is a leverage point.}\n\\label{tab:loo}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Router Training Details}\n\\label{app:router-details}\n\nWe use Qwen3-1.7B for the router to avoid circular dependency with the Qwen2.5-7B data-generating model.\n\nWith gradient accumulation of 1 (effective batch size 4), routing performance is highly sensitive to the evaluation split, suggesting overfitting to specific train/validation partition boundaries. With gradient accumulation of 8 (effective batch size 32), step-level performance stabilizes across splits (${\\sim}54\\%$ balanced accuracy). All reported results use gradient accumulation 8.\n\nUnder step-level evaluation with a fixed data split, cross-seed balanced accuracy ranges from 52.7\\% to 58.1\\%, but internal recall varies from 51.5\\% to 81.8\\%, reflecting threshold sensitivity. However, episode-level evaluation reveals that this apparent discrimination does not generalize: balanced accuracy drops to $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$ across 8 cross-split seeds (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}). Deployed end-to-end, the trained router allocates 97.0\\% of episodes to deterministic computation with 17.6\\% death rate, and misrouting cost (death from internally-routed episodes) exceeds the benefit of correctly routing rare internal-preferred cases.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation methods.} All results use \\emph{generation-based evaluation}: the model generates a routing label as text (\\texttt{enable\\_thinking=False}), parsed for classification. We evaluate at two granularities: \\emph{step-level} (steps from the same episode may appear in both train and validation sets) and \\emph{episode-level} (entire episodes held out). Step-level accuracy ($54.1 \\pm 5.4\\%$) exceeds chance, indicating the router captures some signal, but episode-level accuracy ($47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$, below chance) shows this does not generalize to unseen episodes. A data scaling ablation (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}) rules out step-level data scarcity, pointing instead to a structural limitation: always-deterministic is trivially near-optimal given DAF $= 90.4\\%$. Both metrics are reported in the main text; the model-size ablation (Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}) uses step-level evaluation.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nHyperparameter & Classification Head & Implicit Router \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase model & Qwen3-1.7B & Qwen3-1.7B \\\\\nArchitecture & Linear + LoRA & SFT + LoRA \\\\\nLoRA $r$ / $\\alpha$ & 16 / 32 & 16 / 32 \\\\\nLearning rate & 2e-5 & 2e-4 \\\\\nOptimizer & AdamW & AdamW \\\\\nEpochs & 3 & 3 \\\\\nBatch size (per device) & 4 & 4 \\\\\nGradient accumulation & 8 & 8 \\\\\nWarmup ratio & 0.1 & 0.1 \\\\\nWeight decay & 0.01 & 0.01 \\\\\nMax sequence length & 512 & 512 \\\\\nSeeds & \\{42\\} & \\{42, 123, 456, 789\\} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Router training hyperparameters. Both architectures use the same base model with LoRA adaptation. The classification head degenerates across all configurations; the implicit router results are reported as 4-seed mean$\\pm$std.}\n\\label{tab:hyperparams}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Seed Robustness}\n\\label{app:seed-robustness}\n\nWe assess router generalization at two evaluation granularities: \\emph{step-level}, where steps from the same episode may appear in both train and validation sets, and \\emph{episode-level}, where entire episodes are held out. Both use cross-split evaluation (seeds $\\{42, \\ldots, 49\\}$, 8 seeds total), where the data partition and training seed both vary. Table~\\ref{tab:seed-robustness} reports per-seed results.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nEval.\\ Granularity & Seed & Bal.\\ Acc.\\ (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nStep-level & 42 & 57.06 \\\\\n(same-episode & 43 & 63.99 \\\\\nleakage) & 44 & 55.43 \\\\\n & 45 & 56.14 \\\\\n & 46 & 55.45 \\\\\n & 47 & 50.08 \\\\\n & 48 & 48.03 \\\\\n & 49 & 59.30 \\\\\n\\cmidrule{2-3}\n & Mean$\\pm$std & $54.1 \\pm 5.4$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nEpisode-level & 42 & 48.1 \\\\\n(held-out & 43 & 47.9 \\\\\nepisodes) & 44 & 49.6 \\\\\n & 45 & 49.4 \\\\\n & 46 & 48.5 \\\\\n & 47 & 43.1 \\\\\n & 48 & 45.4 \\\\\n & 49 & 44.8 \\\\\n\\cmidrule{2-3}\n & Mean$\\pm$std & $47.1 \\pm 2.2$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{Always-det (= majority-vote)} & 50.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Per-seed router performance under cross-split evaluation (8 seeds, generation-based, \\texttt{enable\\_thinking=False}). Step-level evaluation allows steps from the same episode in both train and validation sets (same-episode leakage). Episode-level evaluation holds out entire episodes; the resulting $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$ is below the 50\\% chance baseline. A data scaling ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:data-scaling}) confirms this is structural rather than data-limited.}\n\\label{tab:seed-robustness}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe contrast between step-level ($54.1 \\pm 5.4\\%$) and episode-level ($47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$) evaluation reveals a generalization gap: the step-level signal likely reflects memorization of episode-specific context (enabled by same-episode leakage) rather than extraction of generalizable routing features. At episode level, the router performs below chance across all 8 seeds (range: 43.1\\%--49.6\\%). A data scaling ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:data-scaling}) rules out step-level data scarcity as the cause: scaling from 25\\% to 800\\% of the original training set ($32{\\times}$ range) yields no improvement, converging to the always-deterministic baseline ($50\\%$). The failure is structural: with DAF $= 90.4\\%$ and all task types deterministic-majority (86:9 label ratio), always-deterministic trivially solves the episode-level routing problem, leaving no learnable signal for a step-level router.\n\n\\paragraph{Data scaling ablation.} To test whether the episode-level generalization failure stems from data scarcity, we vary training set size from 25\\% to 800\\% of the original via paraphrase-based data augmentation (synonym substitution + template variation on step observations), holding the episode-level validation set fixed. Table~\\ref{tab:data-scaling} reports the results.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nScale & Train samples & Det\\% & Ep.-level Bal.\\ Acc.\\ (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\n25\\% & 433 & ${\\sim}90$ & $49.5 \\pm 0.3$ \\\\\n50\\% & 867 & ${\\sim}90$ & $51.5$ \\\\\n100\\% & 1{,}735 & 89.6 & $49.9 \\pm 0.1$ \\\\\n200\\% & 3{,}470 & 94.2 & $50.0 \\pm 0.0$ \\\\\n800\\% & 13{,}880 & ${\\sim}97.7$ & $51.0 \\pm 1.1$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-det & --- & --- & 50.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Data scaling ablation: episode-level balanced accuracy across training set sizes (25\\%--800\\% of original, $32{\\times}$ range). Internal recall $= 0.0$ at all scales (complete majority-class collapse). Det\\%: fraction of deterministic-preferred labels in training data. The 50\\% scale is a single seed; all others report mean$\\pm$std across seeds.}\n\\label{tab:data-scaling}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll scales hover at ${\\sim}50\\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy---equivalent to the always-deterministic baseline---with no monotonic improvement as data increases. This rules out step-level data scarcity as the explanation for the router's episode-level failure; episode-level minority-class diversity remains a potential confound that cannot be resolved without collecting more internal-preferred episodes. The ground-truth internal-preferred rate (9/245 $= 3.7\\%$) means collecting 50 internal-preferred episodes would require approximately 1{,}350 paired episodes at current environment dynamics---a $5.5{\\times}$ expansion with diminishing return on a $9.6\\%$ signal. The failure points instead to a structural limitation: when DAF $= 90.4\\%$ and all task types are deterministic-majority (86:9 label ratio), the episode-level routing problem is trivially solved by always choosing deterministic, leaving no learnable signal for a step-level router to exploit.\n\nAugmentation disproportionately increases the majority (deterministic) class: Det\\% rises from 89.6\\% at 100\\% to 94.2\\% at 200\\% and ${\\sim}97.7\\%$ at 800\\%. However, the complete collapse to majority-class prediction (internal recall $= 0.0$) occurs even at the smallest unaugmented scale (25\\%, ${\\sim}90\\%$ det), confirming this is not an augmentation artifact. Balanced accuracy corrects for class imbalance by averaging per-class recall, so the metric itself is not biased by the skew.\n\nDecomposition metrics are stable under bootstrap resampling (1{,}000 resamples of the 245-episode dataset): DAF $= 90.6\\% \\pm 4.2\\%$ (95\\% CI $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$); Pearson $r = 0.975 \\pm 0.011$ (95\\% CI $[0.952, 0.995]$). The death avoidance dominance finding is robust to both resampling and split perturbation.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-task-type evaluation breakdown.} To test whether the router learns transferable routing strategies or merely task-type classification, we evaluate existing checkpoints (8 seeds) separately on each of the 11 task types. Of the 11 task types, 7 have \\emph{all-deterministic} labels (no internal-preferred episodes exist), making balanced accuracy undefined---the router has nothing to learn beyond the trivial always-deterministic default. For the remaining 4 task types where both classes are present, balanced accuracy ranges from 50.0\\% to 73.0\\%, but this apparent signal does not indicate cross-task-type routing transfer: the router is effectively classifying task types (which correlate with death risk) rather than extracting a generalizable routing strategy. This complements the data scaling ablation: routing signal does not transfer across task types because the signal \\emph{is} the task type---whether a given task carries death risk. The 4B capacity scaling result (Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}) suggests capacity is one factor, but even 4B's $59.0\\%$ episode-level accuracy does not demonstrate cross-task-type transfer.\n\n\\section{Penalty Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{app:penalty}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n$P$ & DAF($P$) & 95\\% CI \\\\\n\\midrule\n0 & 7.5\\% & [$-36.7$, $83.1$] \\\\\n10 & 50.4\\% & [$19.8$, $94.5$] \\\\\n25 & 70.8\\% & [$48.5$, $97.1$] \\\\\n50 & 82.6\\% & [$67.6$, $98.4$] \\\\\n100\\textsuperscript{*} & 90.4\\% & [$81.5$, $99.1$] \\\\\n200 & 95.0\\% & [$90.0$, $99.5$] \\\\\n500 & 97.9\\% & [$95.8$, $99.8$] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Sensitivity of DAF to death penalty magnitude $P$. DAF$(P) = 1 - \\Delta_{\\text{nd}} / \\Delta_{\\text{full}}(P)$, where $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}(P) \\approx \\delta \\cdot P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$, $\\delta = 0.2653$ is the death-rate differential, and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.84$ (strict neither-died filtering). Bootstrap 95\\% CIs from 1{,}000 resamples. \\textsuperscript{*}Default setting.}\n\\label{tab:penalty-sensitivity}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe non-death score gap $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.84$ is invariant across penalty levels---it depends only on surviving-episode performance, not on $P$. As $P$ increases, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ grows ($16.33$ at $P{=}50$, $29.60$ at $P{=}100$, $56.13$ at $P{=}200$) while $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ remains fixed, mechanically increasing DAF.\n\n\\paragraph{Mechanism boundary: death specificity.}\nThe $r = 0.97$ correlation between per-task death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ is specific to catastrophic death (score $= -100$). Broader failure definitions dissolve the correlation entirely: defining ``failure'' as any non-positive score ($T{=}0$) yields $r = -0.13$ ($p = 0.70$), because non-death episodes show near-zero performance differential between strategies ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.84$). Table~\\ref{tab:penalty-correlation} quantifies this specificity across penalty magnitudes.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rccl@{}}\n\\toprule\n$P$ & $r$ & $p$ & Sig.\\ ($\\alpha{=}0.05$) \\\\\n\\midrule\n10 & $-0.08$ & $0.813$ & No \\\\\n25 & $0.51$ & $0.108$ & No \\\\\n50 & $0.86$ & $0.0006$ & Yes \\\\\n100\\textsuperscript{*} & $0.97$ & $8 \\times 10^{-7}$ & Yes \\\\\n200 & $0.99$ & $1 \\times 10^{-9}$ & Yes \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Pearson $r$ between per-task death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}(P)$ across penalty magnitudes. The correlation is non-significant at low penalties and emerges at $P \\geq 50$, reflecting the transition from non-death-dominated to death-dominated score gaps. \\textsuperscript{*}Default ScienceWorld setting.}\n\\label{tab:penalty-correlation}\n\\end{table}\n\nThis sharpens our central finding: death is not merely the dominant component of routing signal---it is effectively the \\emph{only} component that generates differential routing signal in ScienceWorld. At low penalties, the death contribution is small and $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ reduces to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} \\approx 2.84$, which is near-uniform across task types ($\\text{mean non-death gap} = -1.19$ excluding the \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} outlier); consequently, death-rate differential has no predictive power. As $P$ increases, the death term dominates $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$, and the correlation sharpens because death rates \\emph{are} the performance gap. The DAF values in Table~\\ref{tab:penalty-sensitivity} increase monotonically with $P$, but this is a mathematical consequence of the decomposition ($\\text{DAF} = \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$), not evidence of mechanism robustness.\n\n\\section{Cross-Model Verification}\n\\label{app:cross-model}\n\nTo test whether the death-avoidance finding generalizes beyond Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, we replicate the counterfactual analysis with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on 7 task types ($N{=}70$ episode pairs, 10 episodes per task). The results are strongly consistent with the Qwen findings: 24 episodes (34.3\\%) are deterministic-preferred, \\textbf{0} (0\\%) are internal-preferred, and 46 (65.7\\%) show no preference. The internal strategy dies in 58.6\\% of episodes versus 24.3\\% for deterministic (death-rate differential $+34.3\\%$), compared to $+26.6\\%$ for Qwen2.5-7B. Direction consistency is perfect: all 7 task types show higher internal than deterministic death rates (7/7, compared to 10/11 for Qwen). The larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.6\\%$) and the absence of any internal-preferred episodes (0 vs.\\ 9 for Qwen) suggest that Llama-3.1-8B is more vulnerable to hallucination-induced death, amplifying the death-avoidance effect. These results confirm that the death-avoidance dominance is not specific to the Qwen model family.\n\n\\section{Failure Mode Analysis}\n\\label{app:failure-modes}\n\nThe most common failure mode for the internal strategy is state hallucination: the model predicts that an action succeeded (e.g., ``the water is now boiling'') when the environment state did not change, leading to cascading errors that trigger agent death. The deterministic strategy avoids this by receiving ground-truth observations, but still dies in 16.3\\% of episodes---primarily from selecting inherently dangerous actions (e.g., eating toxic substances) rather than from state misestimation.\n\n\\section{Ablation Studies}\n\\label{app:ablations}\n\n\\paragraph{Capacity scaling.} We scale the implicit router from Qwen3-0.6B to Qwen3-4B and evaluate at both step and episode level (Table~\\ref{tab:capacity}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Step Bal.\\ Acc.\\ (\\%) & Ep.\\ Bal.\\ Acc.\\ (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-0.6B & 52.8 & $50.67 \\pm 2.62$ \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B\\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$} & 54.1 & $47.10 \\pm 2.20$ \\\\\nQwen3-4B & 53.5 & $\\mathbf{58.96 \\pm 1.94}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-det & 50.0 & 50.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Capacity scaling: step-level and episode-level balanced accuracy. Step-level accuracy is flat across scales ($52.8$--$54.1\\%$). At episode level, 4B first breaks the random baseline ($58.96\\%$, 95\\% CI $[55.1\\%, 62.8\\%]$). $\\dagger$\\,1.7B episode-level figure comes from the main experiment (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) with a different training configuration (8 cross-split seeds vs.\\ 4 seeds for 0.6B/4B); direct comparison across scales should be interpreted with this caveat.}\n\\label{tab:capacity}\n\\end{table}\n\nStep-level balanced accuracy is essentially flat across scales ($52.8$--$54.1\\%$), indicating that step-level discrimination is not capacity-sensitive. At episode level, however, the 4B model first breaks the random baseline ($58.96 \\pm 1.94\\%$, 95\\% CI $[55.1\\%, 62.8\\%]$), suggesting that model capacity is one limiting factor for episode-level generalization. Nevertheless, even 4B achieves only ${\\sim}59\\%$---far from a practically useful router---and the dominant explanation remains that death avoidance constitutes a task-type-level binary signal that a step-level router cannot exploit efficiently.\n\n\\paragraph{Reasoning depth.} An ablation comparing chain-of-thought reasoning against direct-answer generation on 6 task types ($n{=}120$ episodes per condition, 6 tasks $\\times$ 20 episodes per task) found no significant difference (Mann--Whitney $p > 0.26$, $|d| \\leq 0.31$) and near-identical death rates (41.8\\% vs.\\ 42.5\\%), confirming that reasoning depth does not meaningfully alter internal-mode outcomes.\n\n\\section{Cross-Environment Analysis: ALFWorld}\n\\label{app:cross-env}\n\nWe evaluate on ALFWorld~\\citep{alfworld-2021}, a text-based household environment with 6 task types (json\\_2.1.1 split, valid\\_seen, 135 games, max 50 steps, binary success metric), using Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Unlike ScienceWorld, ALFWorld has no death penalty---failure means exhausting the step budget. The two modalities differ in \\emph{information access}: the internal strategy receives only free-text observations, while the deterministic strategy additionally receives admissible actions at each step. This is an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch.\n\nThe success rate gap is large: 27.4\\% (deterministic) vs.\\ 3.0\\% (internal), reflecting the 7B model's near-inability to solve household tasks without admissible-action guidance. The label distribution (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-env}) shows 35 deterministic-preferred (25.9\\%), 2 internal-preferred (1.5\\%), and 98 no-preference (72.6\\%), of which 96 are both-fail. The deterministic advantage stems entirely from the information gap rather than death avoidance.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/signal_reversal.pdf}\n\\caption{Label distribution reversal across environments. In ScienceWorld (with $-100$ death penalty), deterministic-preferred labels dominate (86:9). In MATH (no death penalty), the pattern inverts (17:30), with 84.3\\% no preference.}\n\\label{fig:signal-reversal}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nEnvironment & $N$ & Det\\% & Int\\% & NoPref\\% & Death? \\\\\n\\midrule\nScienceWorld & 245 & 35.1 & 3.7 & 61.2 & Yes ($-100$) \\\\\nMATH & 300 & 5.7 & 10.0 & 84.3 & No \\\\\nALFWorld & 135 & 25.9 & 1.5 & 72.6 & No\\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Cross-environment label distribution. $\\dagger$\\,ALFWorld's deterministic advantage stems from action-space pruning, not death avoidance.}\n\\label{tab:cross-env}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\end{document}\n",
      "numLines": 342,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 342
    }
  }
}

  "command": "cat << 'TEXEOF' > /root/autodl-tmp/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/abstract.tex\n\\begin{abstract}\nModular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet understanding \\emph{what} drives the value of routing decisions remains unclear. We propose episode-level counterfactual analysis: running complete episodes under pure strategies and comparing final outcomes to decompose routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. Among task types exhibiting meaningful routing signal in prescreening (11 of 30 available ScienceWorld task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels (75 of 95) are driven by death asymmetry---a penalty-independent metric. Under the default penalty $P{=}100$, the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) is $90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$), though DAF varies from 50\\% ($P{=}10$) to 98\\% ($P{=}500$) across penalty settings (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Per-task death rate correlates strongly with routing signal ($r{=}0.97$, though partly structural as death penalties contribute directly to score differences; \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}). An exploratory step-level router trained on these labels achieves $54.1 \\pm 5.4\\%$ step-level balanced accuracy (with same-episode leakage) but only $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$ on held-out episodes (below the always-deterministic baseline of 50\\%); data scaling ($32{\\times}$) confirms this capacity-dependent limitation; scaling to 4B yields $59.0\\%$ episode-level accuracy ($p < 0.005$ vs.\\ random) but the cost (4B parameters for $+9$\\,pp) is disproportionate to the routing benefit. Cross-environment analysis on MATH ($N{=}300$) shows the death-driven routing signal is not statistically detectable without a death penalty: the label distribution inverts from 86:9 (deterministic-preferred) to 30:17 (internal directionally favored), with a directional trend favoring internal reasoning ($+4.3$ percentage points, $p = 0.079$, not reaching $\\alpha{=}0.05$). A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, 70 episodes) shows direction-consistent death patterns for all task types, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.6\\%$ for Qwen) and zero internal-preferred episodes. These findings indicate that in environments with catastrophic failure modes, routing value is dominated by safety---the routing problem reduces to safety-first modality selection rather than efficiency optimization.\n\\end{abstract}\nTEXEOF",
  "account": "root"
}

{
  "server": "westb-13313",
  "command": "cat << 'TEXEOF' > /root/autodl-tmp/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/discussion.tex\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-environment evidence.}\nDoes routing signal persist without catastrophic failure? On MATH Level~4--5 (300 problems), using a code interpreter as the deterministic modality, internal reasoning directionally outperforms: 81.0\\% vs.\\ 76.7\\% accuracy ($+4.3$~pp, 95\\% CI $[0.0, +8.7]$; McNemar $p = 0.079$, not reaching $\\alpha{=}0.05$). The label distribution inverts: 30 internal-preferred vs.\\ 17 deterministic-preferred (cf.\\ 9:86 in ScienceWorld), with 84.3\\% no-preference. In the absence of death risk, the death-driven routing signal is not statistically detectable---consistent with death avoidance as the primary driver. A complementary evaluation on ALFWorld ($N{=}135$) confirms that the counterfactual framework identifies different routing mechanisms across environments: the deterministic advantage there stems from information asymmetry (access to admissible actions) rather than death avoidance (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-env}).\n\n\\paragraph{Penalty sensitivity.}\nThe $r = 0.97$ correlation is specific to catastrophic death (score $= -100$); defining ``failure'' as any non-positive score yields $r = -0.13$ ($p = 0.70$). The correlation emerges only at $P \\geq 50$ ($r = 0.86$) and reflects that non-death episodes show near-zero performance differential ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.84$; Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Death is effectively the \\emph{only} component generating differential routing signal in ScienceWorld. These findings may be capability-dependent: the 42.9\\% internal death rate reflects a 7B model's hallucination vulnerability; larger models with lower death rates would reduce DAF, potentially revealing non-death routing signal currently masked by catastrophic failures.\n\n\\paragraph{Implications for router design.}\nThe dominance of death avoidance (DAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$) reframes modality routing as primarily a \\emph{safety} problem rather than an efficiency-quality tradeoff. The routing problem reduces to safety-first modality selection---closer to a conservative default policy than to a learned safety critic---aligning more closely with safe RL~\\citep{garcia2015comprehensive} than with cost-quality optimization~\\citep{cogrouter-2026, ares-2026}. The extreme label imbalance (86:9 deterministic-preferred, 150 no-preference) is not an artifact but a central result: among episodes where modality matters, deterministic is preferred at 9.6:1, explaining why always-deterministic is a strong baseline and why the trained router (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) adds no value. A lightweight death predictor may capture most routing benefit without full counterfactual labeling. The capacity scaling results reinforce this: extracting episode-level routing signal requires 4B parameters for only $+9$\\,pp above random ($59.0\\%$), a disproportionate cost that validates our counterfactual analytical methodology as the efficient alternative to brute-force learned routing. In principle, routing could be fully reduced to per-task-type death-risk prediction, bypassing step-level observation entirely---a direction we leave for future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Modality heterogeneity.}\nThe deliberate heterogeneity in deterministic modality definition---ground-truth state observation in ScienceWorld, code execution in MATH, admissible-action filtering in ALFWorld---tests whether the framework (episode-level counterfactual analysis) generalizes, not whether any single modality definition does. The consistent finding across structurally different modalities strengthens rather than weakens the conclusion that catastrophic-failure avoidance is the dominant routing signal wherever it exists.\nTEXEOF",

}

{
  "server": "westb-13313",
  "command": "cat << 'TEXEOF' > /root/autodl-tmp/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/experiments.tex\n\\section{Experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:death-decomp}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-task scatter: death rate differential (internal $-$ deterministic) vs.\\ performance gap ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$). $r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$ task types.}\n\\label{fig:decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAcross 245 episodes, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$; excluding death episodes, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, yielding DAF $= 90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$; at default $P{=}100$). The penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction is 78.9\\% (75 of 95 non-tie labels). The internal strategy dies in 42.9\\% of episodes versus 16.3\\% for deterministic (2.6$\\times$). Per-task, death rate differential correlates strongly with $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\\ref{fig:decomposition}; robustness diagnostics in Appendix~\\ref{app:correlation}). A high correlation is expected whenever the scaled death-rate differential ($P \\times \\Delta_d$) dominates between-task variance---formally, when $\\text{Var}(P \\cdot \\Delta_d) \\gg \\text{Var}(\\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$. At $P{=}100$, this condition holds for all but one task type (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, which has zero deaths). The sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}) confirms that $r$ drops below significance at $P \\leq 25$, where this dominance condition no longer holds. Of 245 episodes, 86 are \\texttt{det\\_preferred}, 9 \\texttt{internal\\_preferred}, and 150 \\texttt{no\\_preference}.\n\nOne exception: \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} ($n{=}40$, zero deaths) has DAF $= 0\\%$ with $\\Delta = +9.58$ entirely from non-death score differences; all 10 remaining types exceed DAF $> 85\\%$. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm $\\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 types; \\texttt{chemistry-mix} ($N{=}20$, $\\Delta = 21.8$, 95\\% CI $[-5.0, 48.6]$) is the sole exception (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task}).\n\n\\subsection{Baselines}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & Score & 95\\% CI & Death \\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\\\\nAlways-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\\\\nRandom & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\\\\nOracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained Router$^\\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 tasks, 245 episodes). $\\dagger$\\,Single seed; see \\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\nAlways-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\\% of decisions matching always-deterministic---\\emph{oracle degeneracy} indicating the optimal routing policy closely resembles unconditional deterministic computation.\n\n\\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic}\n\\label{sec:router-diagnostic}\n\nWe probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. A classification head on LLM hidden states degenerates to always-deterministic across all configurations. An implicit router (Qwen3-1.7B fine-tuned; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) yields $54.1 \\pm 5.4\\%$ step-level balanced accuracy but $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$ on held-out episodes (8 seeds)---below always-deterministic (50\\%). This reveals a capacity-dependent negative: at practical router scales (0.6B--1.7B), episode-level balanced accuracy remains at or below chance ($50.67\\%$/$47.1\\%$). Scaling to 4B yields $58.96 \\pm 1.94\\%$ ($p < 0.005$ vs.\\ random; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations})---confirming that episode-level signal exists but is capacity-starved. Data scaling ($25\\%$--$800\\%$) shows no improvement at any capacity (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}). Notably, scaling model capacity improves episode-level routing ($+9$\\,pp at 4B) without improving step-level accuracy (flat ${\\sim}53\\%$ across scales), confirming that the extractable signal is task-type-level structure---consistent with \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}'s finding that death risk (a task-level property) dominates routing utility. The cost of this extraction (4B parameters for $+9$\\,pp) is disproportionate to the routing benefit, validating our analytical methodology as the efficient alternative to brute-force learned routing. Deployed end-to-end (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}), the router ($-10.70$) does not outperform always-deterministic ($-9.47$; overlapping CIs). We verify label quality in Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}.\n\nA Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, $N{=}70$) confirms direction-consistent death patterns for all tasks, with a larger differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.6\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}). Failure mode and ablation details in Appendices~\\ref{app:failure-modes}--\\ref{app:ablations}.\nTEXEOF",

ToolkenGPT \\cite{toolkengpt-2023} represents tools as special tokens enabling frozen models to invoke massive tool libraries; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} composes heterogeneous modules per subtask; ReTool \\cite{retool-2025} applies RL to discover when code execution is preferable to chain-of-thought.\nThese focus on \\emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \\emph{what signal drives the routing decision?}\nRecent concurrent work sharpens this question: \\citet{tool-overuse-illusion-2026} identify a cognitive-illusion effect in LLM tool use, \\citet{when2tool-2026} show tool-call decisions are encoded in hidden states before reasoning, \\citet{tool-use-tax-2026} quantify a ``tool-use tax'' where tools degrade performance, and \\citet{to-call-or-not-2026} propose a necessity--utility--affordability framework.\nOur counterfactual decomposition provides environment-grounded evidence: in ScienceWorld, $90.4\\%$ of routing signal at $P{=}100$ traces to catastrophic-failure prevention.\n\n\\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}\nCogRouter \\cite{cogrouter-2026} adapts reasoning depth per step, ARES \\cite{ares-2026} selects among reasoning effort levels, Mixture-of-Recursions \\cite{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths, and MTRouter \\cite{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn routing across heterogeneous models.\nRouteLLM \\citep{routellm-2024} learns routers from preference data to select between strong and weak models.\nThese optimize \\emph{how} to route; we reveal \\emph{why} routing helps---in interactive environments, the dominant signal is catastrophic-failure avoidance.\n\n\\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation in RL.}\nPrior work generates counterfactual explanations for RL agents by perturbing individual actions within a fixed policy \\citep{olson2021counterfactual, gajcin2023counterfactual, amitai2024explaining}.\nOur approach differs in both purpose and granularity: we execute two complete episodes under different routing strategies, producing prospective paired comparisons that isolate routing signal \\emph{before} any router is trained, sidestepping compounding perturbation artifacts.\n\n\\paragraph{Safe RL and Catastrophic Failure.}\n\\citet{garcia2015comprehensive} survey safe RL methods that constrain policies to avoid catastrophic outcomes; \\citet{crpo-2021} develop principled constrained optimization with formal convergence guarantees.\nOur counterfactual decomposition reveals that death avoidance is the dominant component of routing signal, suggesting that modality routing functions primarily as a safety mechanism rather than a generic capability-matching problem.\n\n\\paragraph{LLM Agents in Interactive Environments.}\nScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; ALFWorld \\cite{alfworld-2021} provides a complementary household environment; Voyager \\cite{voyager-2023} demonstrates open-ended exploration in Minecraft using LLM-driven curriculum.\nReAct \\cite{react-2023} interleaves reasoning with actions and serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \\cite{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection.\nRecent agent architectures augment planning with world models \\citep{itp-2026, dynathink-2025}, which inherit hallucination risks.\nOur deterministic modality provides exact state transitions, and our analysis shows this exactness matters almost exclusively for death avoidance.\nTEXEOF",

{
  "server": "westb-13313",
  "command": "cat << 'TEXEOF' > /root/autodl-tmp/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/introduction.tex\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nA Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}---a text-based science simulation with 30 task types---faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.\nToolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.\nMore recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \\cite{mtrouter-2026}.\nThe shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources---accuracy, latency, capability matching---and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.\nBut this assumption has never been systematically verified: \\emph{what actually drives the value of routing decisions in interactive environments?}\n\nWe find a surprisingly concentrated answer: \\textbf{death avoidance accounts for 90.4\\% of the routing signal} (DAF $= 90.4\\%$, 95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$; at default penalty $P{=}100$) in ScienceWorld.\nThe internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.\nOnce death episodes are removed, the performance gap between strategies collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$.\n\nReaching this conclusion requires a methodological contribution.\nThe standard approach to generating routing labels---per-step oracle comparison---degenerates under sparse rewards.\nIn ScienceWorld, non-zero reward arrives only at episode termination; 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical scores regardless of modality, leaving no step-level signal to learn from (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\nKeyword-based heuristics also fail structurally, as ScienceWorld actions lack science-specific terminology (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}).\nWe instead propose \\emph{episode-level counterfactual analysis}: run complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compare episode outcomes to generate routing labels (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\n\nAmong task types exhibiting meaningful routing signal in prescreening (11 of 30 available ScienceWorld task types, 245 episode pairs), the full deterministic advantage is $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$ points.\nExcluding death episodes, the gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 78.9\\% of non-tie labels (75 of 95) arise from death asymmetry---a penalty-independent measure.\nThe penalty-sensitive Death Avoidance Factor (DAF; \\S\\ref{sec:daf}) is $90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$) at the default penalty $P{=}100$, ranging from 50\\% ($P{=}10$) to 98\\% ($P{=}500$).\nThe per-task correlation between death-rate differential and performance gap is $r = 0.97$ ($p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$), confirming that death avoidance is the dominant mechanism across task types (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n\nTwo cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.\nFirst, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation (300 problems), using a code interpreter as the deterministic modality, shows a directional trend consistent with the absence of death-driven routing signal: internal reasoning outperforms code interpreter by $+4.3$~pp ($p = 0.079$, not reaching $\\alpha{=}0.05$)---the opposite direction from ScienceWorld.\nIn the absence of death risk, the deterministic advantage is not statistically detectable ($p = 0.079$, not reaching $\\alpha{=}0.05$).\nSecond, a cross-model replication with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (7 task types, 70 episode pairs) shows direction-consistent death patterns for all task types, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.6\\%$ for Qwen; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}), confirming the phenomenon generalizes across model families.\nAn exploratory step-level router (Qwen3-1.7B with LoRA) trained on these counterfactual labels achieves $54.1 \\pm 5.4\\%$ step-level balanced accuracy but only $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$ on held-out episodes---below the always-deterministic baseline---a capacity-dependent limitation confirmed by data scaling ($32{\\times}$) ablations; the 4B scale yields $59.0\\%$ but the cost is disproportionate to the routing benefit (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Routing signal decomposition.}\n    We identify step-level oracle degeneracy in sparse-reward environments and introduce episode-level counterfactual analysis with the DAF metric, a methodology for quantifying what fraction of routing signal derives from catastrophic-failure avoidance (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Death avoidance dominance.}\n    On ScienceWorld, 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels stem from death asymmetry. The penalty-dependent DAF metric reaches $90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ but varies substantially across penalty settings. An exploratory router trained on these labels fails to outperform the trivial always-deterministic baseline on held-out episodes ($47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$ vs.\\ $50.0\\%$); data scaling ablations confirm this capacity-dependent limitation; scaling to 4B yields $59.0\\%$ episode-level accuracy but the cost (4B parameters for $+9$\\,pp above random) is disproportionate when DAF $= 90.4\\%$ and always-deterministic is trivially near-optimal (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-context validation.}\n    On MATH ($N{=}300$), where no death penalty exists, internal reasoning directionally outperforms code interpretation ($+4.3$~pp, $p = 0.079$, not reaching $\\alpha{=}0.05$), consistent with the absence of death-driven routing signal. On ALFWorld ($N{=}135$), deterministic success (27.4\\%) far exceeds internal (3.0\\%), but the mechanism is information asymmetry (access to admissible actions) rather than death avoidance (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}). Both contrasts confirm that the ScienceWorld finding is driven by failure structure, not an inherent advantage of deterministic computation.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nTEXEOF",

{
  "server": "westb-13313",
  "command": "cat << 'TEXEOF' > /root/autodl-tmp/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment $\\mathcal{E}$ with state space $\\mathcal{S}$, observation space $\\mathcal{O}$, and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent follows a policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ that maps observation histories to actions. At each step $t$, the agent receives observation $o_t$, selects action $a_t = \\pi(o_1, \\ldots, o_t)$, and the environment transitions to state $s_{t+1}$.\n\nWe define a modality set $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$ governing how the agent processes state transitions:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Internal} ($m = {\\tt int}$): The agent uses its own generation to predict intermediate observations. The LLM interleaves chain-of-thought reasoning with environment actions following the ReAct paradigm \\cite{react-2023}, but prediction errors compound across steps.\n    \\item \\textbf{Deterministic} ($m = {\\tt det}$): The agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step---equivalent to access to a perfect dynamics model. Action selection remains with the LLM; only the state-transition prediction is replaced.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent We restrict $\\mathcal{M}$ to two modalities. An ablation comparing chain-of-thought reasoning (deep internal) against direct-answer generation (fast internal) on 6 overlapping task types ($n{=}120$ episodes per condition) found no significant difference on any task (Mann--Whitney $p > 0.26$, $|d| \\leq 0.31$) and near-identical death rates (41.8\\% vs.\\ 42.5\\%), confirming that reasoning depth does not meaningfully alter internal-mode outcomes in this setting.\n\nA \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which modality to use at each step. The goal of modality routing is to learn $\\rho$ that maximizes expected episode return. Our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn such a router.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling assigns routing labels by comparing single-step outcomes under each computation modality. Under sparse terminal rewards, this approach degenerates: on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores regardless of modality, yielding no routing signal. We instead perform episode-level counterfactual analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and modality $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying modality $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), ensuring that performance differences arise solely from the modality.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (vLLM) on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is environment-agnostic: it requires only paired episode outcomes under two modalities, with no assumptions about score structure or failure mode. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, reflecting the asymmetric cost of catastrophic failure in interactive environments. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. The full procedure is given in Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}\n\\label{alg:labeling}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, modalities $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$\n\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}\n    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$\n    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = 0$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{both dead}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt det} > s_{\\tt int}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt int} > s_{\\tt det}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $\\ell$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\nThe four mutually exclusive cases correspond to:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Death asymmetry} (lines 5--8): $d^{\\tt int} \\neq d^{\\tt det}$ $\\rightarrow$ the surviving strategy is preferred. This accounts for 78.9\\% of all non-tie labels (75 of 95).\n    \\item \\textbf{Both dead} (lines 9--10): $d^{\\tt int} = d^{\\tt det} = 1$ $\\rightarrow$ ${\\tt no\\_preference}$.\n    \\item \\textbf{Score comparison} (lines 11--14): both survive and $s^{\\tt det} \\neq s^{\\tt int}$ $\\rightarrow$ the higher-scoring strategy is preferred.\n    \\item \\textbf{Tied alive} (lines 15--16): both survive and $s^{\\tt det} = s^{\\tt int}$ $\\rightarrow$ ${\\tt no\\_preference}$.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nLabel distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. The full gap decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation holds exactly when the surviving strategy scores zero in all death-asymmetry episodes; in our data, the residual is small ($29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$, or $0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1, indicating death avoidance overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nDAF is well-defined when $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} > 0$; in our data, the minimum per-task $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ is $+9.58$ (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}), ensuring numerical stability. Since DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$---a mathematical consequence, not an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction.\n\nTo assess sensitivity to penalty magnitude, we recalculate DAF under hypothetical death penalties (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}): $P{=}10 \\rightarrow$ DAF $= 50.4\\%$, $P{=}50 \\rightarrow 82.6\\%$, $P{=}100 \\rightarrow 90.4\\%$, $P{=}200 \\rightarrow 95.0\\%$. Death avoidance remains the dominant component across all penalty magnitudes, though its relative contribution scales with penalty severity. As a penalty-independent companion metric, 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels (75 of 95) are driven by death asymmetry rather than score comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:labeling}), confirming that death avoidance dominates routing signal regardless of penalty magnitude.\nTEXEOF",

}
